An absent config receives `multi_agent_v2 = true`; every existing config remains byte-exact. **Why it matters:** a reinstall cannot infer ownership from history or modify an operator’s existing role, skill, projection, or configuration. **Preserved:** role-policy model and effort selection, delegation rules, external-provider profiles, hooks, and Claude behavior are unchanged. Adoption, update, deletion, reclaim, and migration remain 2.0-only work.

## 2026-07-29

### Fixed

- **`quick-fix` now admits fully specified, reversible work instead of only local additive code.** When targets and steps are known, ownership and contracts are resolved, no dependency or risk owner is added, rollback or backup is explicit, and a verification oracle is named, Lead classifies before task-memory recovery and routes straight to implementation and Quality Assurance (QA), with at most one preflight and one post-verification report. **Why it matters:** a request to back up configuration, update two utilities, rebuild named indexes, and leave an excluded tool untouched no longer falls into research-design-plan loops merely because it is operational. **Preserved:** a failed predicate re-classifies the task; destructive-operation safeguards, verification, QA, and human publication approval still apply.

- **The Codex pack validator now honors the exact maintainer-only status of `shared/references/cross-pack-reconciliation.md` instead of demanding a Russian mirror that standalone provider branches deliberately exclude.** The Russian-localization loop reports that one canonical manifest as an explicit pass and continues to fail closed for every ordinary top-level localizable reference missing its `ru/` counterpart. **Why it matters:** maintainers can run the documented full Codex pack validation after reconciling provider contracts without creating an artifact that the extraction policy does not ship. **Preserved:** the exception is exact-path-only; the top-level localization boundary, provider compatibility checks, and required mirrors for all other localizable documents are unchanged.

- **Closed epics now have a real archive lifecycle instead of remaining indefinitely beside active epics.** Active epic files stay directly under `work-items/epics/`; after Lead records a valid closure, the knowledge archivist moves the same file to `work-items/epics/archive/<YYYY-MM>/`, and reopening restores it to the active root in the same state change. One resolver now distinguishes a unique active epic, a unique archived epic, a missing target, and duplicate locations; the Stop sentinel flags closed-in-root residue, stale or active archived epics, and duplicate slugs, while the periodic checker accepts unique archived links and rejects missing or ambiguous ones. **Why it matters:** location and status can no longer contradict each other silently, so routine documentation hygiene is enforced by the same source rules and checks agents follow. **Preserved:** epic admission, goal evaluation, child roll-up, and closure decisions remain with Product Manager and Lead; only location mechanics and duplicate-safe lookup changed, and existing epic data migration remains an explicit repository-local stewardship step.

- **Delivery-drought enforcement now advances the admitted technical action instead of rewarding process churn.** The existing work-item Stop hook now includes stateless SEN-2: one exact opted-in Primary mutation that remains due after unrelated activity receives one model-facing continuation per root user turn. Delivery credit requires a direct structured exact-target mutation and its same-id explicit-success result; shell text, body mentions, failed, ambiguous, missing, or in-flight results do not count. Invalid input fails open, child and same-turn re-entry contexts are skipped, `HALT` remains absent, and Codex operator-NOTICE limitations are unchanged. Codex and Claude keep the same hook registration identity, so source updates require no new hook trust; SEN-2 has no prose bypass.

### Changed

- **The current Gemini and Qwen full-mirror packs are deprecated and frozen.** They remain selectable only for demonstration, compatibility, and inspection; Codex and Claude are the production lines, and unrelated changes no longer carry Gemini/Qwen parity work. The existing `npm-skillpack-distribution` epic now owns the evidence-backed decision to remove, archive, or replace those mirrors with generated optional targets.

- **Project installers leave the target repository's `.gitignore` entirely under operator control.** Codex, Claude Code, Gemini, and Qwen installers no longer create or modify that file.

## 2026-07-28

### Fixed

- **Correction: Model Context Protocol (MCP) momentum evidence exemptions are repository-rooted, not segment-rooted.** The shared policy now validates the raw hook-envelope `cwd`, finds the nearest plain `.git` directory or file, and applies lexical component-bounded containment only to the four exact root subtrees; a path such as `scripts/work-items` no longer suppresses a code-navigation advisory merely because one segment has an exempt name. Missing, malformed, outside, mixed, or directory-change-ambiguous coordinates grant no exemption, while exact rooted evidence paths remain silent and target paths are never executed, resolved, or required to exist. **Preserved:** the hook remains warn-only, fail-open, exit-0, root/agent-identical, and server-name-only; matcher, registration, dependencies, installer/trust behavior, and the separately authorized installed-dogfood gate are unchanged.

- **Model Context Protocol (MCP) continuity now uses one shared policy across session start, every-turn anchoring, and pre-tool code-navigation warnings on both provider lines.** The existing momentum hook is registered for `Grep|Bash|PowerShell|shell_command|exec_command`, recognizes default-recursive `rg`/`ag`/`ack` and explicit-recursive `grep`, keeps only all-scope `work-items/`, `.reports/`, `.plans/`, and `.scratch/` searches exempt, and applies the same warn-only, fail-open result to root and dispatched-agent envelopes. Advisory output exposes at most three safe matching server names plus a count, never configuration values. **Preserved:** no new hook event, dependency, server, configuration key, or blocking path is introduced; the reminder cannot prove model obedience, and installed dogfood remains a separate post-install gate.

- **Failed or malformed recognized publication-scan executions can no longer mint automatic `tracked` or `range` push credit merely because their body contains a clean receipt.** The single Orchestrarium result-normalization owner now carries provider execution status into the existing correlation pipeline before either receipt classifier. Claude's optional Boolean `tool_result.is_error` is official provider behavior; the leading `Exit code: N` adapter is only observed installed/runtime behavior for Codex 0.145.0 and is not claimed as an official stable Codex application programming interface. An absent or false Claude status and a zero or absent Codex header retain the existing compatibility path, but mean only “no observed failure,” not proven success. **Preserved:** call identity, collision rejection, ordering, scanner failure-marker exclusion, range remote/destination binding, the human approval marker, and the disclosed narrow range residuals remain unchanged.

## 2026-07-27

### Fixed

- **The post-install hook health check no longer fails an entire provider line because ONE third-party entry is registered in a shape this pack does not write.** `scripts/check-hook-health.py` parsed every registered entry's executable shape *before* deciding whether the entry was ours, and Claude's exec form (a `command` plus a string-array `args`) is a shape other tools need not use. A real registration on a live machine — `{"command": "codegraph prompt-hook"}` with no `args` at all, entirely valid for the runtime and simply not this pack's — therefore aborted the run with `FAIL: Claude hook args must be a string array`, reporting a **fully converted, verified-firing 14-of-14 registration as broken**. Ownership is now decided first, and an unparseable entry is **interrogated before it is skipped**: the raw entry is scanned for an owned manifest stem, and an entry naming one fails loudly while an entry naming none is skipped. **The first version of this fix skipped unconditionally, and that was wrong — recorded here rather than quietly repaired, because the wrong rationale was already written into this file.** It claimed that "an entry that cannot be parsed into an argv could never have matched a stem and could never have been ours." Adversarial review falsified it with a reproduction: a command-only entry — `{"type":"command","command":"\"<python.exe>\" \"<abs>/check-bugfix-discipline.py\""}`, no `args` key, precisely the shape the fix's own comment calls valid for the runtime — cannot be parsed on the Claude platform *and yet names an owned stem in its command string and fires this pack's own hook a second time*. Against the operator's real 14-entry registration, the unconditional skip returned **exit 0 and 14 PASS with no duplicate warning**, because a skipped entry never increments its stem's count and so never reaches the duplicate check. Unparseable means *not written in this pack's exec shape* — it does not mean *not ours*. **Why it matters:** this script is the documented post-install verification step in `INSTALL.md` (`python scripts/check-hook-health.py --verify-fires`), i.e. the one instrument an operator is told to trust after installing. A verifier that reports a working installation as broken is worse than no verifier, because the honest response to it is to go re-install something that was already correct. **Who this affects:** every operator running the documented post-install check on a machine where any other tool also registers hooks — which, on Claude Code, is common. **Preserved:** every check applied to an *owned* entry is unchanged; this narrows what the checker polices to what the pack installs, and weakens nothing within that set. The same run also reports leftover `.ps1`/`.sh` wrappers as `WARN`, which is how the un-reclaimed Claude-line wrappers surfaced.

- **A structural contract that had never actually been evaluated now is: `test_trust_guidance_contract` was passing because its own file was untracked, and therefore absent from the `git ls-files` set it scans.** The contract polices every tracked text surface for Codex trust-bypass tokens, requiring each mention to be either a prohibition or a content-hash-pinned provenance block. Committing `tests/test_hook_runtime_installers.py` put the file into its own scan set for the first time, and it immediately reported three hits — all inside itself, because a classifier that detects those tokens must necessarily *name* them. The file already owned the idiom that resolves this (`TOKENS` is built by concatenation so no literal reaches disk); the three remaining literals — the classifier's own token regex, its `codex --dangerously-…` command regex, and the allowlisted prohibition sentence — now use it too, with a class docstring stating why they must never be "tidied" back. **This costs zero coverage, and that is provable rather than asserted:** adjacent-literal concatenation is resolved at compile time, so every runtime value is byte-identical to the unsplit form, and the module's own negative tests — the ones that inject bypass text into synthetic surfaces and assert the contract *fails* — still pass unchanged. **Why it matters:** this is the release's recurring defect class appearing in the guard built to catch it — *a mechanism whose failure is indistinguishable from its success*. The test did not regress when it began failing; it began being evaluated. **The false-confidence window is narrow, and is stated narrowly rather than dramatically:** git history shows this file has exactly one commit ever (`1ad8c5c0`), and checking that commit out in a clean worktree reproduces the failure immediately with the same three hits — so from the first tracked commit onward the contract failed loudly, and no shared or CI-visible suite was ever vacuously green. The vacuous pass existed only in the author's local pre-commit state, which is precisely where the one recorded `1074 passed` run was captured. (An earlier draft of this bullet said "every previous green suite reported a verdict this contract had not actually rendered", implying a sustained shared track record; a claim-check review reproduced the history and refuted it. Corrected here rather than left standing.) **Who this affects:** maintainers reading a suite result as evidence — the post-fix `1074 passed` is the first one in which this contract rendered a verdict at all. **Preserved:** the contract's rules, token set, provenance pins and prohibition allowlist are semantically unchanged; only the on-disk spelling of three literals differs.

- **Hook installs now register the Python hook brains directly, so reinstalling cannot restore the PowerShell-wrapper failure that was already repaired in live operator configuration.** All four production installers resolve one abstract `HookTarget` through the single `resolve_hook_target` runtime selector and pin the absolute `sys.executable` used by that installation process. Before mutation, the Python profile checks every owned hook target and the interpreter's platform contract; the post-registration health gate then actually launches every registered hook, so an unavailable or non-firing runtime fails loudly instead of installing a dead registration. The migration transaction is fixed at **SYNC → REGISTER → VERIFY → RECLAIM**: every installer invokes the same hidden transaction checkpoint at each boundary, while the Python helper alone owns the scratch-contained test-abort policy. Reclaim removes only installed `.sh`/`.ps1` files whose exact names are manifest-owned and whose same-stem `.py` replacement ships beside them; it is last, idempotent, dry-run-visible, and disabled for the explicit wrapper rollback profile. PowerShell failure branches preserve the helper's dedicated exit code instead of allowing terminating diagnostic output to collapse it to exit 1. **Why it matters:** the source installers previously recreated `powershell.exe ... .ps1` entries on every install, undoing the verified live repair and leaving the next reinstall as a regression trigger. **Who this affects:** Claude Code and Codex operators on Windows and POSIX. **Operator workflow:** dry-run and install with the provider installer, then on Codex start interactive `codex` (not `codex exec`), choose **Trust all and continue** for all 13 affected entries, and run `python scripts/check-hook-health.py --verify-fires`; `codex exec` silently skips untrusted entries and the trust modal does not time out. **Preserved:** source wrappers remain in the pack for one-flag rollback; user-owned files and non-hook tooling in the mixed installed directories are excluded.

- **The installer interruption seam now receives the installer's resolved scope explicitly and refuses global mode before an intentional test interruption can fire, even when a test redirects the global home below repository scratch.** One Python boundary parses the ambient request into immutable state; the four Bash and PowerShell installers contain only fixed helper calls for the three public transaction boundaries, while partial reclaim remains Python-owned. Structural tests now parse the Python source and the exact installer function shapes, scan tracked operator/release/publication surfaces for hidden controls, and exercise all four real installers against isolated fake homes with unchanged configuration sentinels. **Why it matters:** a forged test environment can no longer turn a global install into the destructive interruption fixture, and duplicated policy logic in an installer now fails the gate. **Who this affects:** maintainers running focused installer transaction tests; normal installs and wrapper rollback behavior are unchanged.

- **The publication-safety scan gains a `--range` mode, and the git-push gate gains a second, narrower predicate that credits it — closing the case where branch (b) was structurally unreachable, not merely inconvenient.** `tracked` mode's subject is `git diff --cached`; this repository's own governance-prescribed workflow (commit early, run review rounds, push many turns later) leaves that set EMPTY by push time, since the staged index already equals `HEAD` — a scan run there honestly reports "examined 0 files", and the gate's own zero-examined armor (correctly) refuses to credit it. So the only automated leak-check path was dead precisely where the workflow this repository prescribes needs it most, and every push fell to the `[approve-publication]` marker — the one branch that needs no leak-check at all. `check-publication-safety.sh --range <remote> <dst>` scans a DIFFERENT subject instead: the commit set about to be PUBLISHED, `<tip> --not --remotes=<remote>` (`tip` resolved as the current HEAD at scan time), reading content from the COMMITTED BLOB at `tip` — never the working tree, never the index — so it stays meaningful in exactly the workflow `tracked` mode cannot reach. Its receipt (`publication-safety: clean (range, examined N files, remote R, dst D, tip T)`) names the destination it was computed for, and the push gate credits it only when that `remote`/`dst` equal the actual `git push`'s own argv tokens, under the SAME whole-line-anchored, failure-marker-excluded, call-id-correlated, collision-rejected, strictly-ordered machinery `tracked` mode already uses — no new trust primitive, the same one applied to a second regex. **Why it matters:** an operator who commits, then reviews, then pushes several turns later — the pattern this repository's own rules prescribe — can now get a real, automated leak-check credited by a plain-language push instruction, with no manual marker required for that turn. **Who this affects:** every installed project on both provider lines; operators on the commit-then-push-later workflow specifically. **Scope, deliberately narrow — read before assuming more is enforced:** this item was re-scoped down from a much larger design (an exact-spelling push-argv grammar, a literal-SHA refspec-source binding closing a same-command mutate-then-push race, a pinned git-config gate) to exactly what removes the reported pain: the range scan and a remote+dst binding. The wider grammar remains unbuilt and is filed as separate work. **Disclosed residual:** a command that mutates git state (e.g. `git commit`) and pushes in the SAME call can still be credited here as long as `remote`/`dst` match the receipt — a smaller hole than every push falling to the no-scan marker (today's prior, structural defect), but real and not hidden. Repository/worktree identity is still not bound in either mode. **Preserved:** `tracked` mode's own semantics, emitted line text, and zero-examined armor are byte-for-byte unchanged; `--path` mode stays non-creditable; the marker branch is evaluated first and unchanged; the gate's decision path still performs zero subprocesses and zero repository reads.

## 2026-07-26

### Fixed

- **The publication-safety scanner's `password`/`secret`/`token`/`api-key` patterns tested only the variable NAME and stopped at the assignment operator, so any identifier merely ending in one of those words blocked regardless of what followed — reproduced live on an ordinary C# file with no secret present at all** (`CancellationToken cancellationToken = default` blocked because `Token` sits right before `=`; `private readonly string apiKey = config.ApiKey` blocked the same way). Each of the four patterns now requires two independent conditions, both expressed in plain POSIX ERE (`git grep -E`: no `\b`, no `(?:...)`, no lookaround): (1) the keyword must start at an identifier-segment boundary — start of line, a non-letter separator (`_`, `-`, `.`, digit, space, punctuation), or a lower-to-upper camelCase transition — which also closes a pre-existing case-sensitivity gap (`AUTH_TOKEN` in ALL CAPS never matched the old pattern at all); and (2) the right-hand side must show a plausible secret VALUE, not just an operator — either a **delimited** literal (any quoting style: double quote, single quote, backtick) of 12+ secret-alphabet characters or carrying a digit at the bare floor, or a bare literal containing a digit with 6+ total characters **and the digit near an edge** — a digit sitting centred in a 6-to-9-character bare value satisfies neither side's length requirement and is missed, so the effective bare floor is 10 in that case. (That last clause is a correction: an earlier revision of this sentence said "6+ total characters" flat, which overstates the detection strength by omitting the digit-position nuance. The scanner's own comment carries the precise rule.) This is what actually rejects `= default` / `= null` / `= None` / `= nil` / `= undefined` / `= ""` / end-of-line / a member access (`config.ApiKey`) / a call (`await GetTokenAsync(ct)`) — none of those contain a digit, and none are quoted. **Why it matters:** this scanner gates every `git push` in every installed project; a false positive this broad (any C#/Java/TypeScript code merely naming a `Token`-suffixed parameter) makes the gate un-passable on ordinary code, which is worse than not having the gate, because operators learn to route around it. **Who this affects:** every installed project on both provider lines. **Preserved:** every other pattern in the same array (AWS/GitHub/Anthropic key shapes, private-key headers, transcript markers) is unchanged; the scanner's self-exemption for its own catalog lines (keyed to the `check-publication-safety.sh` filename) still holds under the new, longer pattern text, verified directly. **Corrected here rather than left standing:** an earlier revision of this entry said the quoted-value branch matched DOUBLE-quoted literals only, and that a single-quoted secret was caught only via the bare branch. **That was true when written and is now false in both halves — and the second half was never true**: because `'` is in no branch's first-character set, a single-quoted value could not match *any* branch, so a twenty-character digit-bearing secret assigned to a `token` identifier in single quotes passed clean where the old pattern had blocked it. (The literal example is described rather than written out, because the scanner cannot tell prose about a leak from a leak — and this very file blocked its own publication when the example was spelled in full. That is the detector behaving correctly, so the document yields, not the pattern.) An adversarial review caught it as a false-negative regression, and the note itself as a documented compensating control that could not fire. The repair expresses "a delimiter" as a **negated character class** rather than a literal quote, so it matches `'` and a backtick at runtime while the pattern source still contains no `'` — the self-exemption's line shape survives untouched. That class was then **narrowed a second time**: as first written it also admitted identifier and statement punctuation (`_ ( [ { < . / ; , -`), which re-created the original defect through a new route — idiomatic C# such as `apiKey = _configuration.ApiKey;` blocked with no secret present. Measured over the review's own false-positive corpus, the narrowing takes 13 of 15 false positives to 2, with an **identical** catch set on both leak corpora. **Residual, named:** the remaining matchable delimiters are the two quote characters, the backtick, and `! @ # $ % ^ & * ? |`, and a value symmetrically wrapped in one of the exotic ones around a digit-bearing token remains a theoretical vector; none appeared in any of the three corpora across three review rounds. Separately, the value alphabet excludes `@ : $ ! % ~ #`, embedded spaces and non-ASCII, so a secret containing one of those is invisible unless a conforming run precedes it — deliberately not widened, because widening has real false-positive cost. **What this does NOT close:** a sibling design document (`work-items/active/2026-07-26-push-gate-range-receipt/design.md`) that quotes real scanner-catalog output as evidence still blocks the scanner — an unrelated, unconditional PEM private-key header pattern in the same catalog fires wherever the document quotes that specific catalog line verbatim — a separate blocking source this fix does not touch.

- **The work-items sentinel registry ships with only two invariants and two response tiers — SEN-2 (delivery drought) and the `HALT` tier were both withdrawn before release, on live measurement that their entire operator-facing channel does not exist on the Codex line.** Two probes (T-14, T-20; full evidence in `references-codex/stop-hook-halting-primitives.md`) measured that a Stop hook's `stopReason` and even a **bare** `systemMessage` — no `continue`, no `decision` — do not reach the operator on Codex, in any payload shape, confirmed genuine (not a false negative) via a `fired.log` execution proof, six independent runs across three `CODEX_HOME` configurations, and `--dangerously-bypass-hook-trust` ruling out the earlier fresh-install trust gate that had made an initial attempt inconclusive. `HALT` (`{"continue":false,"stopReason":...}`) was removed first (T-14), since a run-terminating tier silent to the operator on one of its two lines is undetectable, not merely unattributed. SEN-2 was cut second (T-20), because its entire release rationale depended on its `NOTICE` reaching *someone* — T-20 showed it reaches nobody on Codex either. **Read this before relying on this registry's output reaching a human on Codex, or covering what its name implies:** (1) only `RESOLVE` (`decision:"block"`) is genuinely cross-line, because it addresses the model, not the operator; (2) every operator-directed output this registry can produce — the `stop_hook_active` escalation `NOTICE` and the sentinels-unavailable fallback `NOTICE` (FM-1) — is Claude-line only; (3) there is no run-terminating tier on either line; (4) a persistent RESOLVE (SEN-0 or SEN-1) forces a fresh model continuation every user turn, FOREVER -- it does not go silent: `stop_hook_active` is a WITHIN-turn flag that resets at the first Stop fire of every new user turn (measured False on the first of nine envelope fires), so a still-holding finding re-fires to the model at the start of every subsequent turn, costing one continuation each time; the escalation to a NOTICE only happens in the narrower within-turn double-fire case, and even then reaches nobody on Codex, so the net effect there is a per-turn model tax with no operator signal at all, never a one-time notice that then falls silent; (5) FM-1 itself is undeliverable on Codex, so if `workitem_sentinels.py` ever fails to import on that line, the entire guard can vanish with zero operator-visible signal — on the same line the originating incident happened on; (6) **a spiral without a resurrection emits nothing at any volume** — SEN-1 addresses this item's originating incident only because that incident happened to also include a resurrection, not because it is a general spiral guard: measured, a spiral WITH a resurrection fires day 0, the identical spiral WITHOUT one produces nothing at 900 files and still nothing at 5,000 files, so an item that only ever accumulates file residue (instance (B), never a dual-state resurrection) is UNADDRESSED by this release. **The sum of the above, plainly: on the line the incident happened on, this release adds no enforcement that survives a non-cooperating session; its value there is the extension seam plus a per-turn model prompt.** **Why it matters:** the 2026-07-25 entry above originally described SEN-2/`HALT` as shipping; they never did, and this entry documents why, together with the disclosure now written into `src.codex/AGENTS.codex.md`, `references-claude/claude-md-structural-enforcement.md`, and `INSTALL.md` so an operator or hook author reading any of the three sees the same six facts — the coverage limit (6) as plainly as the delivery-channel limits (1-5), not softened into "limited coverage". **Who this affects:** every installed project on both provider lines — Codex operators specifically need points 1-5 above to reason correctly about what this guard can and cannot tell them. **Preserved:** SEN-0 and SEN-1 (the invariant that directly addresses this item's own originating incident) are unaffected; hook-entry counts, the wrapper filename, and installer behavior are unchanged. **Re-proposal:** SEN-2's underlying problem (an item accumulating file residue with no matching delivery signal) is re-proposed on a different substrate — a turn/spend counter rather than a git-commit-attribution threshold — as decision `2026-07-26-delivery-drought-needs-a-substrate-not-a-threshold`.

- **The bounded-reverse-scan transcript reader (`hook_common.last_genuine_user_text`) is a 92-line addition (43 executable), not the "~30 lines" estimated in an earlier informal note, and its own regression suite (`tests/test_workitem_sentinels.py::TestG14T16BoundedReverseScan`, `TestG16T18HookCommonAdditivity`) confirms `read_transcript_tail` stays byte-identical (a 92-line, zero-deletion diff on `hook_common.py`).** Measured speedup on a real 418 MB transcript: 2.1 ms (bounded reverse scan) versus 3009.8 ms (a full-file read), roughly 1420×. **Why it matters:** this helper's blast radius is now stated precisely rather than estimated, and its continued presence is re-justified for SEN-0's F3 operator-channel widening rather than its original SEN-2-clearing purpose, which no longer exists. F3's own blast radius is corrected in the same pass: losing it does not cost "one extra turn" (a RESOLVE re-fires every turn regardless, per the disclosure above) — it costs only the operator's own ability to clear the finding directly by typing the marker themselves; without it, the model must write the marker itself (T2, always available either way) — a quality-of-life gap, not a safety-critical one. **Who this affects:** anyone reviewing this change surface's diff size or performance characteristics. **Preserved:** the helper's behavior, callers, and the four other hooks that use the byte-unchanged `read_transcript_tail` are untouched.

- **KNOWN GAP in this release, stated here rather than only in a commit body.** Two shipped reference documents — `references-claude/claude-md-structural-enforcement.md` and `src.codex/AGENTS.codex.md` — still describe the six audits as writing a stderr warning and exiting 1, which **contradicts the code shipped in this same release** (stdout JSON advisory, exit 0). Worse, the Codex one hedges the exit-1 visibility question as `ASSUMPTION (UNVERIFIED)` when this batch's own measurement already **refuted** it — a hedge on a measured-false claim, in installed governance. A reader of either document will be taught the mechanism this release replaced. Tracked as `2026-07-26-reference-docs-still-teach-the-disproven-exit-1-visibility-premise` and owed as a completion obligation on the audit-delivery fix; it is called out here because the commit that aligned the other contracts is titled as if it aligned these too.

- **Review rounds are now counted at the dispatch, so the shipped 3-round cap can actually fire.** The cap has existed as text; one family in this batch ran **eight rounds and nothing fired**, because the contract's own enforcement depends on a ledger that the same actor who exceeds the cap is the one who would have written — and, as the shipped text already admitted, *no mechanism observes that ledger's absence*. The observer therefore hooks the subagent-dispatch `PreToolUse` event, which fires at the decision moment whether or not anyone recorded anything. It keys on `(operator-turn boundary, subagent_type)` — both harness-written, neither reword-able — and warns at 4, 8, 16, 32. **A prompt-text key was built first and killed by measurement:** over 350 real dispatches it produced 13 false clusters, the largest reporting 44 "rounds", and the reason is the finding — median within-role consecutive path overlap is **0.00**, because genuine rounds legitimately reword themselves. A text key is not merely evadable; it is false-negative-prone against a cooperating actor. **No state is stored** — depth is recomputed per call by a bounded reverse read (measured p95 6.7 ms at 100 MiB against 446 ms for a whole-file control), because a counter file would be one more artifact whose absence nobody observes, which is the defect being repaired. **Who this affects:** Claude-line projects only. **What this does NOT cover, and neither gap is closed:** Codex has **zero** coverage — no equivalent dispatch tool exists there, and the incident that motivated this work happened on that line; and rounds separated by operator turns reset the key, so a family spread across several of your messages is not counted. Whether the eight-round family above would have fired is **unestablished**, and is recorded that way rather than assumed favourable. WARN-only by design: the hook knows the count and cannot know the artifact, while the model knows the artifact and provably does not track the count.

- **A growth gate for the Codex skill catalog now fails before an overflow ships.** `scripts/validate-codex-skill-catalog-budget.py` warns at 80% and fails at 90% of the catalog ceiling, so the budget announces itself instead of being discovered after the fact. Maintainer tooling; no installed behaviour changes.

- **All six warn-only `PreToolUse` audits now deliver their warning to the MODEL, via `hookSpecificOutput.additionalContext` with exit 0 — the previous `exit 1` + stderr channel was measured to reach the model on NEITHER runtime, so for their entire shipped life these six audits warned nobody.** The audits exist to steer the model's next action; every one of them wrote to stderr and exited 1, on a documented premise that a non-zero, non-2 exit surfaces a transcript notice. Measured on Claude Code 2.1.220 and Codex CLI 0.145.0: that notice reaches the **operator**, never the model, and on Codex the non-2 branch never copies stderr at all, so the warning reaches nobody whatsoever. The replacement (`hook_common.emit_advisory`) prints a JSON advisory and exits 0; it reads the event name **from the incoming envelope first**, because a `hookEventName` that does not match the firing event makes Claude discard the entire payload silently — the single likeliest way to reintroduce this. **Why it matters:** this is the defect class the whole release repairs, in the mechanism built to detect that class: six controls whose failure was indistinguishable from success, invisible to six green audits because every one of them checked registration, execution, and predicate logic — never delivery. **Who this affects:** every installed project on both provider lines. **Preserved:** all six stay AUDIT-mode — they always allow the tool call, still fail open, and still never exit 2. **What this does NOT cover, stated plainly:** committing this fix is necessary and **not sufficient**. The audits that run are the *installed* copies, and verifying the source is not verifying the control — an install step must follow, and until it does the six audits stay mute on any machine carrying an older install. An install-freshness assertion comparing source against installed hashes is owed and not in this change.

- **The git-push publication gate now requires a publication-safety scan's clean result to arrive under the SAME tool-call identity as the invocation — not merely that a scan ran and clean-shaped text appeared somewhere in the turn.** This closes two defects in sequence. First: step 8 branch (b) originally opened on scan *invocation* alone, so a scan that examined nothing — the ordinary case after a commit, when the staged index already equals `HEAD` — read as a pass, reproduced live in this repository. The first correction made the scanner self-report a result line, but joined call-evidence and result-evidence as two independent strings across the whole turn: an adversarial review then demonstrated live that an empty-index scan plus an unrelated `Read` of a file quoting the scanner's exact clean text — **a test fixture in this repository's own suite did** — made the gate ALLOW with no real clean scan anywhere. **What ships is identity-based, not textual:** the clean-result text must be carried by a tool output whose call-identity field matches the id of the tool call that matched the scan pattern (Claude `tool_use.id` ↔ `tool_result.tool_use_id`; Codex `function_call.call_id` ↔ `function_call_output.call_id`). Those fields were read from **real session logs on this machine**, not from provider documentation, because provider-runtime documentation had already been measured wrong twice this session. **An earlier revision of this entry claimed the mechanism was "fail-closed by construction". An external review disproved that, with executable fixtures on both provider shapes, and the claim is corrected here rather than quietly dropped.** Identity matching alone was a bare set-membership test: it could not distinguish *one call owns this id* from *two calls share this id*, so a duplicated identifier let an unrelated call's output — carrying the clean-result text — open the gate. What ships now **rejects ambiguity instead of resolving it**, on both sides independently: a call id claimed more than once cannot count as the scan invocation, and an output id claimed more than once cannot count as that scan's answer. Neither side picks a winner by any heuristic; an ambiguous id is worthless as evidence. **Uniqueness is counted over every id-carrying call, not only shell ones** — that distinction is the whole correction. An earlier attempt scoped uniqueness to command-carrying calls, the same extractor used to *detect* scans, so a non-shell call sharing a scan's id was structurally invisible to it; when the scan's own answer never arrived, the one remaining output under that id was the foreign one and the gate opened. **What makes an id ambiguous is any second claimant, of any tool type; what makes a call a scan invocation is its command text.** Conflating those two scopes was the defect, and separating them is the fix. Verified over **24,530 real tool calls** (no call shape skipped by the extractor) and **4,257 real turns** (no turn newly denied), with pre/post mutation controls on both provider shapes. Ordering is enforced too — the credited result must appear **strictly after** its call, since a result that precedes a call is not an answer to it. The joined-haystack helpers were **deleted** rather than kept as a fallback, so no text-matching path remains to silently degrade into. **Preserved:** `[approve-publication]` is untouched and checked first; every documented fail-open path is unchanged; `git push --dry-run` is still always allowed. **What this does NOT cover, named rather than implied:** no worktree, repository, or destination binding — this repository alone runs six live worktrees of itself, and a scan correlated in one worktree with a push issued in another within the same turn is not distinguished by anything here; multi-commit pushes, force pushes, rebases, amends and mirror/tag pushes still fall entirely to the operator marker; a model can still forge the evidence by echoing the clean text under a real call id, so this defends against momentum and accident, not an adversary with a shell; commit messages are still never scanned by any path.

- **A dead provider run is now detected on the next poll — one 25-second interval by default — instead of a 45-60 minute blind wait.** `invoke-{codex,claude}-prompt.{sh,ps1}` write a `<slug>.pid` sidecar, and `await-codex-dispatch.{sh,ps1}` gained a `DEAD` terminal status (exit 69) reached only when the recorded process is confirmed gone **and** no completion artifact exists. A PID-recycling guard compares the recorded process start marker, so a reused identifier classifies as dead rather than alive. **What the shipped default actually delivers is detection on the next poll, and the default poll interval is 25 seconds** — measured 25.494 s end-to-end, against 0.534 s when the interval is tuned down (`await-codex-dispatch.sh:87-89,260`, `.ps1:69-71`). The sub-second figures describe a tuned watcher, not the shipped one. The improvement is real and large — one poll interval instead of a 45-60 minute blind wait — but it is bounded by the polling cadence, not by the probe. The suite pins only `< 5 s` (Bash) and `< 10 s` (PowerShell) with a tuned interval (`tests/test_active_codex_dispatch_watcher.py:472,670`). **Preserved, stated precisely:** a missing or malformed handoff degrades to the pre-existing artifact-only resolution (`DONE`/`STALL`/`TIMEOUT`) and its **stdout tokens are unchanged** — which is the common case, and was the incident's own case, since that loop launched by hand. It is **not** byte-for-byte identical overall: the same release deliberately made terminal statuses distinguishable by exit code, so a `TIMEOUT` that previously exited 0 now exits 124. An earlier revision of this entry claimed byte-for-byte equivalence without qualification; that conflated the handoff's degradation with the exit-code change shipped alongside it. **Disclosed asymmetry, not hidden:** Bash captures the real provider child process; PowerShell captures the wrapper's own, because its call operator runs a `.ps1` in-process and no separate child exists.

- **`await-codex-dispatch.{sh,ps1}` exit codes now distinguish outcomes a caller previously could not tell apart**, and two adjacent silent-failure surfaces are closed. Every terminal status used to exit 0, so a delivered review and a 45-minute stall were indistinguishable by exit code alone; `STALL` now exits **75** (`EX_TEMPFAIL`) and `TIMEOUT` **124** (matching `timeout(1)`), while `DONE` stays 0 and usage errors stay 2. **Stdout tokens are unchanged; the exit codes are not** — a caller that previously treated any exit 0 as success will now see 75 or 124 where it saw 0, which is the entire point but is a behavioural change, not a purely additive one. An earlier revision of this sentence called it purely additive while the neighbouring entry named that same conflation as false; the two now agree. The run-completion oracle's fatal-marker scan was anchored to a no-timestamp shape and missed the **timestamped** fatal lines this runtime's transport actually emits, so a run whose transport died mid-stream read as complete; the pattern now covers both, verified against two independently-found real samples plus seven falsifying controls, on Windows PowerShell 5.1 and PowerShell 7. All four pack validators carried a masked PowerShell 5.1 hazard where a `cmd.exe` AutoRun hook's incidental stderr, promoted to a terminating exception under `$ErrorActionPreference = 'Stop'`, made the validator proceed against the wrong fallback root **and report success**. **Why it matters:** each of the three was a mechanism whose failure was indistinguishable from normal operation — a stall that exits like a success, a fatal that scans like a clean run, a validator that validates the wrong tree and passes. **Residual, disclosed rather than dropped:** the fatal pattern does not cover non-`Z` timezone offsets or lowercase severity tokens, neither observed in any real sample, and the same AutoRun idiom remains in the four monorepo installers, which sit outside the swept provider trees and are filed separately.

- **Both PowerShell prompt wrappers dropped their `param()` block, closing a silent-drop/hard-error defect on any model or effort override passed via `-File`.** PowerShell's binder resolves an unambiguous **prefix** of a declared parameter name — and, once `ValueFromRemainingArguments` is present, also the built-in common parameters — before capturing remaining arguments. So Codex's own `-c` (mandatory for `-c model_reasoning_effort=<tier>`) collided with the declared `-CodexFlags`, and Claude's `-p` with `-PromptFile`: measured to either strip the flag marker or hard-error, meaning a caller following the wrapper's **own documented usage** could not pass the provider's real short flags at all. Both wrappers now parse `$args` by hand with **exact**, not prefix, matching for their own control flags; every other token forwards byte-for-byte, in order. **Why it matters:** the guard requiring an explicit model/effort pin on every provider-backed run was itself unreachable from the wrapper's documented Windows invocation whenever the override needed a provider short flag — fixed at the root (the binder) rather than per-flag. **Preserved:** all recognized flag names, the guard's logic and messages, ledger event shape, the `TopicSlug` filesystem-boundary check, and both Bash siblings, whose exact-match `case` loop has no analogous collision surface. The former `-Command` + array-literal workaround is retired. Regression coverage reproduces the exact collision shape and asserts byte-for-byte argument fidelity — an earlier version of the fixture **masked the bug it was meant to catch**, and was rewritten.

- **The local-only `.gitignore` tier writer, duplicated across all eight installers, can no longer confuse "never configured" with "considered and declined."** An operator who deliberately tracks a tier (say `/work-items/`) may leave the sentinel `# orchestrarium:local-only-tier-declined:/work-items/` in place of that tier's line; the writer checks it with the **same whole-line matcher** used for tier presence, so the two can never disagree about one file. Real runs now report each tier they add, and a declined tier is reported as declined rather than silently skipped. **Why it matters:** the writer was quoted elsewhere as an escape hatch — "a project that deliberately removes it keeps it removed" — which was false; it is now true. The probe that decides this is hardened against ambient git configuration: `core.excludesFile` is set explicitly on the throwaway probe repository, closing a leak where git's fallback path (`$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/git/ignore`, else `~/.config/git/ignore`) could make an unrelated global file decide the answer. **That hardening step's own failure was originally discarded** — `|| true` on the Bash side, a `catch` that never checked `$LASTEXITCODE` on the PowerShell side — while its immediate neighbour, a failing `git init`, *was* checked. An external review forced the failure rather than reasoning about it, and the installer still returned 0 while the ambient ignore leaked into the probe: the leak this step exists to close, silently restored. All eight installers now treat that write as a precondition and fail closed exactly as the `git init` failure already did, so a probe that cannot be hardened is reported as unusable rather than trusted. That fallback fires precisely when the key is unset, so clearing environment variables alone could not suppress it. Verified on Bash, PowerShell 7.6.4 and Windows PowerShell 5.1, each with a control and a falsification probe. **Preserved:** default behaviour for every operator who has not opted out is unchanged, and a genuinely missing tier is still appended exactly as before. **Also fixed:** the example-only Gemini and Qwen pack validators gained the bundled-shell PATH fallback, WSL-launcher rejection, and null-safe `git rev-parse` already shipped for the Claude/Codex pair — reversing the 2026-07-19 entry's deliberate exemption. Both packs remain example-only and `WEAK MODEL / NOT RECOMMENDED`; this is maintainer tooling, not installed behaviour.

- **Shipped design-panel and review-loop text now says what the mechanism actually does, in four places where it claimed more or less than it delivers.** Both installed design-panel bindings regained DP3's third sentence — the one answering "is the same engine running twice independent?" — which had dropped out of both while the provider-neutral trunk kept it, leaving a runtime operator with no installed text to answer the question they actually face. The review-loop trunk and its two bindings, which had drifted **in both directions**, now agree: the trunk gained hardening invariants 7-8 and the model-mismatch trigger its bindings already carried, and both bindings gained the FROZEN-artifact rule, ledger read-back, and close-every-open-`runId` rules only the trunk carried. The `review-loop-state` ledger requirement **stays a `MUST`** — a proposed downgrade to "convention" was gated, returned `REVISE`, and reverted, so net normative force versus the previous release is **zero**; what was added instead is a disclosed envelope naming the real residual: **no shipped mechanism observes the ledger's absence**, because the validator's only input is the record and the invariants are applied in-session by the same actor who would have written it. That is measured, not feared — a failing session called its fail-closed ledger helper **705 times, passed every time, then stopped calling it**, under this same `MUST`. Closing it needs a runtime-authored observer, tracked as decision `2026-07-26-missing-mechanism-disclosed-not-obligation-relaxed`. Alongside: the same-vendor-versus-external split is now explicitly distinguished from independence at all three sites keying on it; the Claude design-panel command gained the sequential-fallback instruction its Codex sibling already had; the lead skill's "four concurrent agents" default is labelled `ASSUMPTION (UNVERIFIED)` with a resolving probe on both lines; `INSTALL.md` documents that Qwen project installs write the local-only tiers, matching behaviour the installer already had; and `src.codex/skills/external-reviewer/SKILL.md`'s failed-run citation, which pointed at a path that does not exist, is repointed at the real owner.

- **A propagation mechanism for the three byte-identical script and hook mirrors now exists — `python scripts/sync-universal-hooks.py --check|--sync`.** Before this, `tests/test_universal_hook_surfaces.py` could only *detect* drift; nothing propagated a fix, so every lane hand-copied an edit to "the other two" trees from memory — the exact gap that produced this session's near-miss, where a lane's edits landed in one tree, the other two stayed stale, and a narrower run reported a false-green against the unedited copies, caught only by that lane's own manual re-diffing. The canonical direction is **stated, not inferred**: `scripts/universal-hooks/` is the source, on evidence from the parity test's own single-owner comment, `shared/references/repository-source-hygiene.md`, decision `2026-07-11-hook-placement-gate-semantics`, and an unrelated validator that independently calls the same tree the pack-neutral canon. `--sync` never guesses which side of a conflict is right: it asks **git** whether a drifted mirror carries its own uncommitted changes — not mtime, which resets on checkout — and refuses with an explicit `CONFLICT` rather than overwrite real work. Fail-first verified with fixtures: ordinary drift propagates; a mirror edited directly and left uncommitted is refused untouched; two independently diverged mirrors are each refused on their own, neither destroyed. The manifest (canon name-lists plus declared pack-only exceptions) was extracted into a new **dependency-free** module, `scripts/universal_hooks_manifest.py`, imported by both the test and the CLI, so a defect in the CLI's own more volatile code can no longer take the parity gate down at collection — which happened exactly once during this change, when a `dataclasses`/Python-3.14 interaction crashed collection of the whole file, and is now caught as one named, attributable test instead. **Why it matters:** the previous state was a passing test run that proved nothing, and it was invisible from inside the lane that produced it. **Who this affects:** any lane editing a mirrored hook or script across the Claude and Codex packs. **Preserved:** the parity test's byte-for-byte and set-equality assertions are unchanged in substance, and no mirrored script's shipped behaviour changed. **Does not cover:** automatic conflict resolution — by design, a mirror carrying real uncommitted work is always left to a human; and the tool was deliberately never run with `--sync` against the live mirrors while they were still in flux during its own development.

- **`scripts/maintenance/cleanup.py`'s CLI gained an explicit `--now` clock seam, ending a test that passed today and failed on a date nobody chose.** Thirty-six direct-API assertions froze a clock via `scan_valuables(..., now=NOW)`; the seven that went through `main(argv)` had no equivalent path, so they silently used the real wall clock and one began failing once enough real days passed (observed `age=9.64d` against an intended 1-day-old fixture). Root cause confirmed by arithmetic, not guessed. **Why it matters:** it also cleared a live failure that had already caused a concurrent lane to misattribute the cause to an unrelated environment-clock issue. **Preserved:** every existing invocation without `--now` is behaviour-identical, and the API contract, fallback-age gate, and git-uniqueness predicate are unchanged. **Not addressed here, tracked separately:** the `check-scratch-valuables.py` SessionStart watchdog is an independently hand-duplicated mirror of the same age logic and is untouched — it rounds ages to one decimal where `cleanup.py` rounds to two, one more measured divergence, and whether the two agree remains unverified.

## 2026-07-25

### Fixed

- **The Codex operator-facing binding no longer claims Codex has no subagents, or that any hook-script edit requires re-trusting every hook.** Two stale or over-broad claims in `src.codex/AGENTS.codex.md` are corrected against the installed `codex-cli 0.145.0` runtime (source verified at the matching tag `rust-v0.145.0`). First (`:177`), the routing section previously stated "every chain runs in the main session ... Native Codex skill execution is sequential" as if that were a runtime limit; the installed runtime carries `SubagentStart`/`SubagentStop` hook events (whose envelopes require `agent_id`/`agent_type`), `CollabAgentTool` variants (`spawn_agent`, `send_input`, `resume_agent`, `wait`, `close_agent`; `codex-rs/protocol/src/items.rs:250-256`), and stable `multi_agent`/`multi_agent_v2` features — confirmed both at source and in the installed binary's own string table. The corrected text keeps the pack's own routing POLICY unchanged (the main session stays the owner; `$lead` is never dispatched as a subagent) while no longer misdescribing the runtime under it. Second (`:113`), the manual hook-trust instruction previously said re-trust is required after "any hook script or hooks.json command" change; Codex's trust hash is built from a normalized hooks.json **entry** identity (event, matcher, command string, timeout, async flag, status message, context limit) and never covers the hook script file's own contents, so editing a wrapper's script body while its filename and hooks.json command line stay byte-stable re-trusts nothing. The corrected text also documents that trust is keyed per entry by a literal, lexically-normalized-but-not-canonicalized path string (a bare case difference produces an unrecognized key, i.e. `Untrusted`) and that appending a new entry never disturbs an earlier entry's key or hash. **Why it matters:** both claims were load-bearing premises in an in-flight architecture review (`work-items/active/2026-07-25-review-round-cap-enforcement/`) before being caught false; left uncorrected in the shipped doc they would keep misleading any operator or hook author who reads it. **Who this affects:** Codex operators and anyone authoring or upgrading a Codex-line hook. **Preserved:** hook entry-point paths, the installer's fresh-install auto-untrusted behavior, and the existing "when in doubt, review all" conservative fallback advice are unchanged; hook counts, installer behavior, and hook-script behavior are untouched — this is a documentation-only correction.

- **New `references-codex/stop-hook-halting-primitives.md` (with a Russian mirror) documents that `decision: "block"` at the `Stop` hook event is a forced continuation, not a halt, on both the Claude Code and Codex lines.** Measured directly this session: the same one-word probe task against a `Stop` hook returning `{"decision":"block","reason":...}` produced `num_turns: 10`, 9 hook fires, and 18 assistant messages, while the same setup returning `{"continue":false,"stopReason":...}` produced `num_turns: 1` and a clean `stop_hook_prevented` termination. `continue: false` takes precedence over `decision: "block"` on both provider lines (confirmed at Codex source, tag `rust-v0.145.0`, including the upstream unit test `continue_false_overrides_block_decision`), and on the Claude line `stopReason` is documented as shown to the operator and explicitly not shown to the model. The same file records that the `stop_hook_active` envelope flag is advisory metadata each hook must honor itself, not an enforced runtime cap — measured `True` on 8 of 9 fires in the `decision:"block"` run while the loop still reached ten turns. **Why it matters:** a `Stop` hook author reaching for `decision:"block"` to end a run gets the opposite of a halt; the correct primitive is `continue:false` (+ `stopReason`). **Who this affects:** anyone authoring a new Codex or Claude Code `Stop` hook meant to genuinely stop a run. **Preserved:** this pack's own shipped `Stop` hooks (`check-passive-polling-stop`, `check-work-items-archival-stop`) intentionally keep using `decision:"block"` for a bounded model-facing correction — that choice is unchanged; only the new reference doc is added.

- **`src.codex/skills/lead/SKILL.md:8` and `src.codex/skills/review-loop/SKILL.md:14` no longer ground a routing policy on the runtime-capability claim the correction above just refuted.** `skills/lead/SKILL.md:8` previously read "Codex loads roles as in-context skills, not recursive subagents, **so** the orchestration owner stays in the main session" — the `so` grounded a correct, durable policy (one context owns delegation, gate integrity, and artifact acceptance) on a capability premise that `AGENTS.codex.md:177` (see above) had just shown false, shipping a direct self-contradiction to every Codex user in the same install. The clause is re-grounded on its policy reason instead; the `$lead`-is-never-a-subagent rule and the pinned "the main session IS the lead" sentence are preserved verbatim. `skills/review-loop/SKILL.md:14` is corrected per clause: the claim that Codex "does not spawn three concurrent in-process subagents" is refuted by the installed runtime's own V1 tool description ("Parallel delegation patterns": "spawn multiple agents for them in parallel" — `codex-rs/core/src/tools/handlers/multi_agents_spec.rs:741-745`) and V1's own thread cap (`effective_agent_max_threads` resolving `V1 | Disabled` to `agent_max_threads`/`DEFAULT_AGENT_MAX_THREADS` — `codex-rs/core/src/config/mod.rs:1463-1477` — a cap that presupposes concurrent threads), so the loop's use of the external-dispatch/external-brigade surface is now stated as the file's own strength-aware default assignment (both verdict scopes dispatch into a fresh external process via `$external-reviewer` — the deep-reasoning scope through ordinary `auto`, the surgical-correctness scope through the explicit `externalProvider: codex` self-provider override — so neither verdict is ever cast by the session that authored or is revising the artifact) rather than a capability workaround. **Withdrawn:** an initial correction instead grounded this on cross-vendor judgment independence ("a native subagent cannot supply a genuinely different vendor's judgment"); an independent architecture review caught that this self-contradicted the same file's own invariant seven lines below (`:22`, "Angles are defined by SCOPE, not by vendor ... Vendors are symmetric") and the shared trunk (`review-loop-methodology.md:11`), so it is corrected here to the scope/strength-aware grounding instead, and the design-panel entry immediately below is corrected to match. The unverified claim that a native `spawn_agent` run "fires completion notifications" is left standing under an explicit `ASSUMPTION (UNVERIFIED)` rather than silently rewritten. **Known limitation (tracked, not corrected in this pass):** four maintainer-only reference-doc sites still assert the same now-refuted claim — `references-codex/subagent-operating-model.md:11` and its `ru/` mirror, plus `references-codex/operating-model-diagram.md` (6 sites) and its `ru/` mirror (5 sites; the two mirrors are not symmetric) — and the Codex validator (`src.codex/skills/lead/scripts/validate-skill-pack.sh:1117-1120`) currently *requires* the stale phrase "sequential skill invocation" to remain present in `subagent-operating-model.md`, with a SHA-256 fingerprint pin at `:1183-1185` over that same stale file. Correcting those sites requires rewriting that validator assertion in the same change, so it is deliberately scoped out of this pass (`references-codex/` is maintainer-only, never copied to target projects — `INSTALL.md:80`) and left for a follow-up work-item. **Why it matters:** the shipped, user-facing self-contradiction is closed now; the maintainer-only residual is lower-radius but still a documented false claim, and the validator itself currently ratchets it in place, until the follow-up lands. **Who this affects:** Codex operators (fixed here) and repository maintainers reading `references-codex/` (still stale). **Preserved:** no hook, count, or installer surface changed by this entry; both pack validators stay green.

- **`src.codex/skills/design-panel/SKILL.md:14` carried the identical now-refuted claim and is corrected in this same bounded follow-up — with its own grounding, not a copy of `review-loop`'s.** That skill's own `DP3` invariant states independence comes from framing and sealed fresh contexts, **not vendor count**, and the shared trunk (`shared/references/design-panel-methodology.md`) is explicit that "different vendors with the same framing are not independent" — so the cross-vendor rationale used for `review-loop` cannot transfer here regardless of whether it holds there. **Withdrawn:** an earlier draft of this entry cited `spawn_agent`'s own tool description unconditionally (`codex-rs/core/src/tools/handlers/multi_agents_spec.rs:761,765`) — "a spawned agent can message its spawner and other running agents by canonical task name." An independent architecture review found that sentence lives ONLY in the tool-description variant gated by the host's `multi_agent_v2` feature flag (`Stage::Stable`, `default_enabled: false` — `codex-rs/features/src/lib.rs:1055-1059`); the default-configured variant (`multi_agent`, `default_enabled: true` — `codex-rs/features/src/lib.rs:1049-1053`) resolves to `MultiAgentVersion::V1` (`codex-rs/core/src/config/mod.rs:1434-1452`) and carries no such channel, so the unconditional claim was false on the default install. The flag is also **sufficient but not necessary**: `multi_agent_version_for_model` (`codex-rs/core/src/config/mod.rs:1454-1461`) falls through to the active model preset's own `multi_agent_version` (`codex-rs/protocol/src/openai_models.rs:239,633`) whenever the flag override is unset, so a preset can select V2 with the flag never touched — the skill text now names both surfaces rather than the flag alone — the "valid for `review-loop`" clause in an earlier draft of this entry was likewise incorrect and is withdrawn. **The corrected grounding:** whether the cross-lane channel exists depends on the host's `multi_agent_v2` flag, a runtime configuration the skill does not condition its guarantee on; the candidate lanes route through the external-dispatch / external-brigade surface instead because the failure modes differ, not because a guarantee can be made unconditional — an unchecked native cross-lane channel would break `DP3`'s seal silently, while an external lane that cannot run fails visibly into `DP7`'s `UNVERIFIED` / `BLOCKED:dependency`. The now-vestigial `ASSUMPTION (UNVERIFIED)` aside about native completion notifications is dropped from the skill text along with it. A phrasing-family sweep found no further in-scope shipped skill site carrying the original defect. **Why it matters:** a skill that grounds a routing guarantee on a runtime capability claim tells the operator that something structural protects them; when the claim is only true under a configuration the skill never inspects, the operator is relying on a protection whose actual condition is invisible to them. **Who this affects:** Codex operators using `/design-panel` and `/review-loop` (both fixed here). **Preserved:** the design-panel invariants (`DP1`-`DP8`), every validator assertion for this file, and the "Known limitation" residual immediately above (unchanged, still four Tier B sites) are untouched.

### Added

- **The `Stop` hook `check-work-items-archival-stop` is now a per-event SENTINEL REGISTRY (`workitem_sentinels.py`) hosting two invariants, not a single check — the pack's answer to "the pack has no extensible always-on invariant surface" (`work-items/active/2026-07-25-review-round-cap-enforcement/design.md`).** SEN-0 (archival orphan) is the original behavior, migrated verdict-equivalent (the pre-existing `tests/test_work_items_archival_hook.py` suite — 40 tests / 120 subtests — passes unchanged against the new adapter), with its bypass marker `[acknowledge-open-work-items]` now also read from the operator's own last genuine typed message, not only the model's last reply. SEN-1 (dual-state item) is new: a work-item slug present in BOTH `work-items/active/` and `work-items/archive/**`, checked across the union of the working tree and `HEAD`, so a slug whose archive copy was deleted from disk but still exists in a tracked commit is still caught — reproduced live against the incident repository, which independently flagged its actual resurrected slug (`2026-07-13-fortran-port-injection-generalization`). Both invariants answer with `RESOLVE` (`decision:"block"`, costs one model turn, unchanged primitive for the cheap/model-fixable case) or `NOTICE` (`systemMessage` alone, costs no model turn — used only for a tier-escalation rule: a RESOLVE-tier finding suppressed by `stop_hook_active` additionally emits a turn-free `NOTICE` naming the still-unresolved invariant). **A third invariant (SEN-2, delivery drought) and a third response tier (`HALT`, `continue:false`) were designed and then withdrawn before release** — see the 2026-07-26 entry below for why and what that means for every invariant this registry hosts, on both provider lines. **Why it matters:** the root failure this item traces was a session that abandoned a working, fail-closed ledger gate — called it 705 times, then simply stopped — so no gate keyed on the ledger, `status.md`, or any other model-authored signal can be trusted; every invariant here is sorted by an authorship lattice (T0 runtime/T1 user/T2 model-free/T3 model-at-a-cost) and reads only T0/T1/T3 signals, never T2, in its detection logic (one declared, scope-narrowed T2 exception: `[acknowledge-open-work-items]` clears SEN-0 alone, never SEN-1). **Who this affects:** every installed project on both provider lines, transparently — the wrapper's FILENAME is unchanged (Codex hashes the hooks.json entry, never the script's contents, so this upgrade re-trusts nothing on an already-trusted install), no installer was edited (both installers copy their containing directories wholesale), and net hook-entry counts stay unchanged (Claude 14, Codex 13). **Preserved:** the cap-3 review-round-escalation value at all nine sites, `agent-run-ledger.py` (unmodified, and never a sentinel input), and the sentinel/validator split (`workitem_sentinels.py` never imports `check-work-items-state.py`/`validate-work-item-state.py`; the validator now OPTIONALLY reports sentinel findings informationally via a one-way import, never the reverse). **Residual, not closed in this change:** on a Codex-line install with no prior `hooks.json`, the whole surface (like all 13 existing entries) stays inert until a one-time interactive trust approval; `check-scratch-valuables` (the pack's other always-on repository-state gate, on `SessionStart`) is a tracked, declared exception to the registry, migrating in a follow-up (`2026-07-25-migrate-scratch-valuables-to-sentinel-registry`).

## 2026-07-20

### Added

- **New Claude-only `check-typed-routing` warn-only PreToolUse audit flags a `general-purpose` dispatch doing typed specialist work.** The Claude pack now ships a tenth structural hook (`src.claude/agents/hooks/check-typed-routing.{py,sh,ps1}`): a warn-only, fail-open audit that fires at a subagent-dispatch when `subagent_type` is the built-in catch-all `general-purpose` AND the dispatch prompt carries a specialist-work signal (implementation / review / design / security / performance / toolchain), pointing the orchestrator at the matching typed pack role. It never blocks (exit 1 on a hit so the nudge is visible, exit 0 otherwise, always allows) and skips subagent contexts (`agent_id`). The dispatch-tool shape was captured from real session transcripts before wiring — `tool_name` is `Agent` (not `Task`), with `subagent_type` in `tool_input` — and the hook keys on those named constants and stays INERT if the shape is ever absent, so a future rename makes it fire nothing rather than a false block. **Why it matters:** a `general-purpose` dispatch for specialist work silently bypasses the typed roster and its gate; the audit surfaces that at the one observable moment, the dispatch decision. **Who this affects:** Claude Code operators; the installer auto-registers it on the `Agent` matcher (opt out with `--no-hypothesis-hook`). **Boundaries:** Claude-only — Codex CLI has no analogous subagent-dispatch tool, so there is no Codex mirror and the Codex pack's hook count is unchanged (Claude now installs fourteen entries / ten structural hooks / six audits; Codex stays thirteen / nine / five). Promotion to a blocking `deny` remains a separate step after the false-positive rate is measured.
- **Review-loop contract now excludes a sidecar completion signal from counting as a liveness verdict.** Hardening invariant 5 ("Verify OUTPUTS, not notifications") in the Claude review-loop contract, the Codex review-loop skill, and the shared review-loop methodology trunk now states that a completion signal from a sidecar (watcher / notifier / background-task callback / "task done" notification) is NOT a liveness verdict on the process it watches — liveness of a launched run is proven only by a direct probe of the run itself (its PID/exit status, or its own `.out`/`.err` carrying a normal-completion marker), never by a neighboring task's completion. **Why it matters:** it closes the proxy-liveness misattribution where a watcher's "done" was read as the watched run's completion. **Boundary/deferred:** the parallel always-on spine binding is deferred — `shared/AGENTS.shared.md` is at 39,898 of its 39,900-char cap (2 chars of headroom), so the clause could not be added without a separate zero-sum compaction pass; it currently binds through the review-loop surfaces only.
- **`/agents-status` now surfaces reserved-but-unfired `$product-manager` admissions and offers an operator-confirmed dispatch.** A new "Reserved PM admissions" section scans the three live cross-cutting registries (`work-items/decisions/`, `work-items/epics/`, `work-items/bugs/`) and flags an artifact only when it BOTH co-locates a `$product-manager` admission/acceptance call (an action stem from `{admit, accept, intake, re-intake, pending, call}` on the same or an adjacent line) AND carries a non-terminal authoritative frontmatter `status:` (decisions `proposed`, epics `active`, bugs `open`); when the count is greater than zero it appends one dispatch-offer line. **Why it matters:** reserved product-manager calls — an epic unpark, acceptance of a `proposed` cross-cutting decision, admission of a new initiative — previously had no triggering entry point, so a scheduled PM dispatch silently never fired and the item stalled; the dashboard operators already run now makes those reserved calls visible and actionable. **Who this affects:** operators running `/agents-status`. **Preserved boundaries:** the command stays read-only (it flags and offers but never dispatches a subagent or mutates a file); any dispatch is operator-confirmed and performed by the main conversation, not the command; the `OR direct human decision` admission bypass is intact; the flag reads only the authoritative frontmatter `status:` key (never a stale body substring), excludes `work-items/archive/**` and `## Backlog` items, and is a strict subset of the existing proposed-decisions list rather than a duplicate of it.

## 2026-07-19

### Fixed

- **The always-loaded Claude Code instruction entrypoint now carries only compact operative hook rules, with exhaustive structural-enforcement detail preserved losslessly in the canonical Claude maintainer reference.** `src.claude/CLAUDE.md` decreased from 60,400 Unicode code points / 60,742 UTF-8 bytes to 36,081 code points / 36,271 bytes while retaining Bootstrap, delegation, routing, bypass, role, publication, diagnostic/publication ownership, current-turn safety-scan, Stop ownership, audit, reminder, and Model Context Protocol (MCP) rules. `scripts/validate-claude-md.py` now enforces the conservative 36,271 binding size with an exact 500-unit guard (hard cap 36,771), an inclusive warning band beginning at 36,521, and 47 grouped lose-nothing manifest pins. Four source payloads remain byte-identical under unique delimiters in `references-claude/claude-md-structural-enforcement.md`, which `references-claude/README.md` now indexes. **Who this affects:** Claude Code operators load substantially less provider-specific context on every session, while maintainers get one canonical long-form hook reference plus focused size, rule, payload-hash, pointer, and line-ending regression guards.

- **Installed Claude-line prompt wrappers now find their sibling agent-run ledger helper, and both Windows pack validators can use a shell resolved from `PATH`.** All four Bash and PowerShell prompt wrappers probe the installed sibling `agent-run-ledger.py` before their two existing monorepo candidates, so `--ledger` / `-Ledger` works in the layout produced by the installer. The Claude and Codex PowerShell pack validators keep bundled-shell discovery under the Git-derived root as their first choice, then probe `bash` followed by `sh` through `Get-Command` only when every derived-root candidate is absent. Existing monorepo fallback order, derived-root precedence, and fail-closed behavior when no helper or shell can be resolved remain unchanged. **Who this affects:** operators running installed prompt wrappers with ledger recording or invoking either pack validator from a Windows environment where Git resolves through `mingw64\bin` while Bash remains available on `PATH`.

- **The six shell-resolving PowerShell launchers now find the bundled Git Bash whether `git` resolves through `cmd\git.exe` or `mingw64\bin\git.exe`, and their `PATH` fallback rejects both Windows Subsystem for Linux (WSL) launcher locations, so the Windows publication leak-check gate and both pack validators can no longer miss the bundled shell or select a bash that cannot run them.** On a Git-for-Windows install `git.exe` exists at BOTH `...\Git\cmd\git.exe` and `...\Git\mingw64\bin\git.exe`, and `Get-Command git` returns whichever is first on `PATH`. Each launcher derived its bundled-bash search root as two levels up from `git.exe`: from `cmd\git.exe` that is `...\Git` (which has `bin\bash.exe`), but from `mingw64\bin\git.exe` it is `...\Git\mingw64`, which has NO bundled bash — so the derived-root probe missed and control fell through to the `PATH` fallback. **Root fix:** the candidate list now ALSO probes the grandparent root (`Split-Path -Parent $gitInstallRoot`), so `...\Git\bin\bash.exe` is found in the `mingw64` case (three levels up) as well as the `cmd` case (two levels up); the existing two-levels-up candidates stay first, so nothing regresses and the WSL-prone fallback no longer runs for a normal Git-for-Windows layout. **Defensive fallback fix:** when the fallback does run, it now enumerates every `PATH` candidate and rejects any whose resolved path is under the Windows directory (`$env:SystemRoot` / `$env:windir`, with a literal `C:\Windows` fallback when both are unset, matched as a path PREFIX in either separator form — one structural rule that covers `System32`, its 32-bit-process `Sysnative` view, `SysWOW64`, and any future Windows-dir launcher variant) OR under a `Microsoft\WindowsApps` execution-alias directory (a `[\\/]`-separator regex, since that alias lives under the user profile rather than the Windows dir) — the WSL `bash.exe` launcher homes, none of which can resolve `C:\Users\...` paths — and selects the first non-WSL `bash` then `sh`; when every candidate is a WSL launcher it fails closed to the existing bundled-bash `throw` rather than running WSL bash. The Windows-dir PREFIX rule deliberately REPLACES the earlier per-alias `System32|Microsoft\WindowsApps` substring list (which had grown one alias at a time — `System32`, then the Store `WindowsApps` alias, then `Sysnative`), so a newly-surfaced Windows-dir launcher variant needs no further per-name rule. `tests/test_powershell_wrappers_smoke.py` gains a root-fix regression (a `mingw64`-style git whose grandparent holds the bundled bash must resolve the derived-root shell, never the `PATH` fallback), a WindowsApps-rejection regression, and a `Sysnative`-rejection regression — each with a genuineness proof that DISABLES the single arm under test in a byte-level copy of the launcher (with the WindowsApps arm disabled the alias, planted outside the Windows dir, is selected; with the Windows-dir prefix arm disabled the `Sysnative` launcher is selected — proving each arm is load-bearing, not a tautology of the other) — all across all six launchers under Windows PowerShell 5.1 and pwsh. The `Sysnative` fixture points `$env:windir` at a synthetic Windows dir (overriding `$env:SystemRoot` would crash Windows PowerShell 5.1 startup, so only `windir` is repointed), exercising the real prefix code path without touching the real Windows dir. A follow-up re-consilium cleanup GATES the grandparent probe on `$gitInstallRoot`'s LEAF being a mingw layer (`mingw64` / `mingw32` / `usr`) rather than the interim `if ($gitParentRoot)` guard: the normal `...\Git\cmd\git.exe` layout has leaf `Git`, so the grandparent is not probed and an unrelated `<grandparent>\bin\bash.exe` in a nonstandard git layout can never be mis-selected; the leaf test is also non-empty by construction, so it subsumes the drive-root guard (a `git.exe` two levels below a drive root such as `X:\cmd\git.exe`, whose `X:\` install-root leaf is not a mingw layer, never enters the branch and so never crashes on an empty-string `Join-Path`).
